Maximal regularity and optimal control for a non-local Cahn-Hilliard tumour growth model


Abstract
We consider a non-local tumour growth model of phase-field type, describing the evolution of tumour cells through proliferation in presence of a nutrient. The model consists of a coupled system, incorporating a non-local Cahn-Hilliard equation for the tumour phase variable and a reaction-diffusion equation for the nutrient. First, we establish novel regularity results for such a model, by applying maximal regularity theory in weighted L p L^{p} spaces. This technique enables us to prove the local existence and uniqueness of a regular solution, including also chemotaxis effects. By leveraging time-regularisation properties and global boundedness estimates, we further extend the solution to a global one. These results provide the foundation for addressing an optimal control problem, aimed at identifying a suitable therapy, guiding the tumour towards a predefined target. Specifically, we prove the existence of an optimal therapy and, by studying the Fréchet-differentiability of the control-to-state operator and introducing the adjoint system, we derive first-order necessary optimality conditions. Key words: Non-local Cahn-Hilliard equation, Well-posedness, Maximal regularity, Optimal control, Tumour growth. AMS (MOS) Subject Classification: 35K61, 45K05, 35B65, 35Q92, 49K20, 92C50.



Matteo Fornoni
1

e-mail:
matteo.fornoni01@universitadipavia.it

1
Department of Mathematics "F. Casorati", Università degli Studi di Pavia,

via Ferrata 5, 27100, Pavia, Italy

1
Introduction

Mathematical modelling and analysis of tumour growth models is an expanding field that has been attracting much research in recent years.
Indeed, it encompasses both modelling and numerical challenges, related to applicability with medical data (see
[
1
,
8
,
6
,
25
,
33
,
34
]
), and analytical questions, related to well-posedness and regularity results (see
[
20
,
43
,
29
,
27
,
40
]
).

In the present paper, we consider a diffuse interface tumour growth model, which is a non-local variant of the one firstly introduced in
[
30
]
.
In particular, it is a Cahn-Hilliard-based tumour growth model: for a general introduction to this type of models, we refer the reader to the recent review
[
24
]
.
The key feature of diffuse interface models is the presence of a phase-field variable
\varphi
, representing the difference in volume fractions of tumour cells and healthy cells in a certain tissue.
We assume that such variable can vary continuously between the tumour phase
\varphi=1
and the healthy phase
\varphi=-1
.
Such approach, as opposed to a sharp-interface one, allows for an easier description of the tumour dynamics, both from an analytical and numerical point of view; additionally, topology changes in the tumour level set are also possible.
In particular, the main object of our study is the following model:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi-\divergence(m(\varphi)\nabla\mu)=P(\varphi)(\sigma+\chi(1-\varphi)-\mu)-\mathbbm{h}(\varphi)u\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }Q_{T},

\displaystyle\mu=AF^{\prime}(\varphi)+Ba\varphi-BJ\ast\varphi-\chi\sigma\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }Q_{T},

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma-\divergence(n(\varphi)\nabla(\sigma+\chi(1-\varphi)))=-P(\varphi)(\sigma+\chi(1-\varphi)-\mu)+v\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }Q_{T},

\displaystyle m(\varphi)\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}}\mu=n(\varphi)\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}}(\sigma+\chi(1-\varphi))=0\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma_{T},

\displaystyle\varphi(0)=\varphi_{0},\hskip 10.00002pt\sigma(0)=\sigma_{0}\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega,

where
Q_{T}=\Omega\times(0,T)
,
\Sigma_{T}=\partial\Omega\times(0,T)
and
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
N=2,3
, is an open bounded sufficiently smooth domain, with exterior normal unit vector
\boldsymbol{n}
.

We now briefly comment on the structure of the system.
Other than
\varphi
, the second main variable of the system is
\sigma
, representing the concentration of a nutrient (e.g. oxygen or glucose), which is assumed to be the main consumable source used by the tumour cells to proliferate.
As one can see, the phase-field variable
\varphi
satisfies a non-local Cahn-Hilliard equation, whereas
\sigma
satisfies a reaction-diffusion equation, and the two equations are non-trivially coupled through reaction terms and cross-diffusion ones, due to chemotactic effects.
The non-locality is given by the convolution term
Ba\varphi-BJ\ast\varphi
in the equation for the chemical potential
\mu
associated to the Cahn-Hilliard equation.
Throughout the paper, we call
a=J\ast 1
(see also the forthcoming hypothesis
A2
).
Including non-local effects is of paramount importance, for instance when modelling cell-to-cell adhesion effects (see
[
10
]
).
The expression of
\mu
, as well as the form of the non-local terms, is deduced from the variational derivative of the Ginzburg-Landau free energy associated to the system:

\begin{split}\mathcal{E}(\varphi,\sigma)&=\int_{\Omega}AF(\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\Omega}\int_{\Omega}\frac{B}{4}J(x-y)(\varphi(x)-\varphi(y))^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y\\
&\qquad+\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}\lvert\sigma\rvert^{2}+\chi\sigma(1-\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}x,\end{split}

(1.1)

where
A,B>0
are parameters related to the width of the diffuse interface.
We note that, without reaction terms and sources, the system above can be seen as an
H^{-1}
-gradient flow of the previous free energy.

With this in mind, the first term in the free energy drives the evolution of the phase-field towards the minima of the function
F
, which is typically taken as a double-well potential with equal minima in
-1
and
1
.
Typical choices for such potential are for instance

\displaystyle F_{\text{reg}}(s)=\frac{1}{4}(1-s^{2})^{2},\hskip 10.00002pts\in\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle F_{\text{sing}}(s)=\frac{\theta}{2}\left[(1+s)\log(1+s)+(1-s)\log(1-s)\right]-\frac{\theta_{0}}{2}s^{2},\hskip 10.00002pts\in(-1,1),\hskip 10.00002pt0<\theta<\theta_{0},

From a modelling point of view, the best one would be the singular potential
F_{\text{sing}}
.
Indeed, its minima are strictly inside the interval
(-1,1)
, but sufficiently close to the extrema, and its derivative blows up in
\pm 1
.
This still allows to restrict the evolution inside the physical interval
[-1,1]
, but also enables to show the existence of regular solutions, as long as a proper separation property is proved (see
[
26
]
for a recent overview on the matter, as well as
[
41
]
).
In a few words, this amounts to proving that
\varphi
stays strictly inside the interval
(-1,1)
during its evolution, thus allowing free differentiation of the potential
F_{\text{sing}}
.
As such property is not always easy to prove, in many cases a suitable choice of the potential is the polynomial one
F_{\text{reg}}
, which, albeit not imposing the physical constraint, still typically guarantees good results in practice.
Going back to the free energy (
1.1
), the second term is the one responsible for the non-local character of the model.
Indeed, given a sufficiently regular symmetric convolution kernel
J
, typically of Newton or Bessel type, it can be seen as a non-local approximation of the standard Dirichlet energy that one would find for the local Cahn-Hilliard equation.
From the modelling viewpoint, this term wants to penalise steep transitions between the two phases, in a non-local way.
Indeed, if the kernel
J
is suitably peaked around zero, one can also recover the local model through asymptotic procedures (see
[
14
,
16
,
15
]
).
Applications of the local Cahn-Hilliard equation to tumour growth problems have been widely studied, as one can see from the many articles already cited in this introduction, but the study of the non-local counterpart, where also some long-range interactions are considered, is just recently flourishing (see
[
25
,
10
,
44
]
).
Our paper fits exactly in this framework.
To conclude our commentary of the free energy (
1.1
), the third term is responsible for the nutrient diffusion mechanism and the fourth one for the chemotaxis effect, which is related to the natural movement of tumour cells towards regions with higher nutrient concentrations.
Here we model chemotaxis through a cross-diffusion effect with intensity
\chi\geq 0
, but more sophisticated models are also available, using for instance the celebrated Keller-Segel equations (see
[
43
]
).

Now, going back to our system, we comment on the remaining parameters.
The functions
m(\varphi)
and
n(\varphi)
are called mobilities and regulate the diffusion processes of the two variables.
In some cases,
m(\varphi)
can be taken degenerate in
\pm 1
, like
m(\varphi)=1-\varphi^{2}
, to compensate the singularities of the potential
F_{\text{sing}}
(see
[
23
,
21
]
), but here we will work with the constant mobility case.
The function
P(\varphi)
, instead, is a proliferation function, calibrating the strength of the reaction terms, which, in turn, are written in this form due to chemical phenomenological laws (see
[
30
]
).
Generally, it can be taken of the form
P(s)=\max\{0,\min\{\frac{1}{2}(1+s),1\}\}
, for
s\in\mathbb{R}
, especially in case of young tumours, but other choices are also possible.
To conclude,
u
and
v
are external sources that can be thought as medical therapies on the tumour: in particular,
u
can be seen as radiotherapy acting directly on tumour cells and
v
as a chemotherapy acting through the nutrient.
In this case, the function
\mathbbm{h}
is a bounded function that can be used to distribute the radiotherapy through particular strategies.
In most cases, its expression would be similar to the one of
P
.

The main purpose of this work is to analyse the previously introduced model with constant mobilities and regular potential.
Indeed, we now assume
m(\varphi)=n(\varphi)\equiv 1
for simplicity and consider
F
to be a regular potential defined on the whole
\mathbb{R}
, satisfying certain hypotheses for which
F_{\text{reg}}
is certainly included (see the forthcoming hypotheses
A3
–
A5
).
Hence, we actually consider the following system:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi-\Delta\mu=P(\varphi)(\sigma+\chi(1-\varphi)-\mu)-\mathbbm{h}(\varphi)u\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }Q_{T},

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mu=AF^{\prime}(\varphi)+Ba\varphi-BJ\ast\varphi-\chi\sigma\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }Q_{T},

(1.3)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma-\Delta\sigma+\chi\Delta\varphi=-P(\varphi)(\sigma+\chi(1-\varphi)-\mu)+v\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }Q_{T},

(1.4)

together with the following homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions and initial conditions:

\displaystyle\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}}\mu=\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}}(\sigma-\chi\varphi)=0\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma_{T},

(1.5)

\displaystyle\varphi(0)=\varphi_{0},\hskip 10.00002pt\sigma(0)=\sigma_{0}\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega.

(1.6)

Our first main result concerns the existence and uniqueness of highly regular solutions to (
1.2
)–(
1.6
). To achieve this, we use an approach based on maximal regularity theory in weighted
L^{p}
spaces for parabolic equations with inhomogeneous Robin boundary conditions, as was recently done in
[
23
]
for a non-local Cahn-Hilliard equation with singular potential and degenerate mobility.
The theory we apply was developed by M. Meyries and R. Schnaubelt in the series of papers
[
37
,
38
,
39
]
, as well as the PhD thesis
[
36
]
of M. Meyries.

To the best of our knowledge, this is the first time that such results are applied to a Cahn-Hilliard system with regular potential and constant mobilities, especially in presence of reaction terms, chemotaxis, external sources and space-dependent terms.
In particular, the same procedure also guarantees new regularity results for the standard non-local Cahn-Hilliard equation with constant mobility and regular potential (see Remark
4.10
).
Indeed, other than the application of maximal regularity theory, the crucial point is to prove that weak solutions are globally bounded through an Alikakos-Moser iteration technique.
On this point, we mention that this is an intrinsic problem related to the choice of a regular potential in the non-local Cahn-Hilliard equation.
Opting for a singular potential, instead, would replace such problem with the hurdle of proving the aforementioned separation property.
Great advances were made in
[
41
]
, where such property was proved for the non-local Cahn-Hilliard equation in three space dimensions.
Moreover, such results were later used in
[
42
]
to prove regularity results similar to ours in case of an advective non-local Cahn-Hilliard equation with constant mobility and singular potential.
Let us mention, however, that their same procedure would not work in case of our tumour growth system, since the same proof of the separation property cannot be easily adapted to the presence of reaction terms.
Finally, we observe that the Alikakos-Moser iteration scheme for our tumour growth system works only if we neglect the chemotaxis effect, i.e. we put
\chi=0
, since the low regularity of weak solutions is not enough to treat the cross-diffusion terms (see Proposition
4.1
).
Thus, we are actually able to prove
local
maximal regularity and uniqueness for the full model (see Theorem
3.10
), but we are able to extend the local maximal solution to a
global
one only if
\chi=0
(see Theorem
4.7
).
We stress that the local maximal regularity result of Theorem
3.10
holds also in the standard non-weighted case, that would be
\rho=1
in the setting of Section
3
.
However, one of the main ingredients for the extension to a global regular solution is the time-regularising effect of the weighted spaces, which allows to prove the key estimate contained in Lemma
3.11
.
For this reason, it is crucial to have maximal regularity in the time-weighted spaces to then recover a global regular solution even in the non-weighted case.
To conclude this section about regularity, we just mention that our analysis was deeply inspired by
[
23
]
, but in this work we chose to deal with the different case of regular potential and constant mobility.
However, we strongly believe that all results on the tumour growth model should also easily extend to the case of singular potential and degenerate mobility as in
[
23
]
.
In particular, also the application to the optimal control problem should be possible, by mimicking what was already done in
[
22
]
.

Speaking of optimal control problems, we now introduce the second main goal of this paper, as well as the key reason why we strived to prove the existence of such regular solutions.
Indeed, highly regular solutions of the tumour growth model are essentially needed if one wants to characterise the solutions to the optimal control problem through optimality conditions.
In particular, the main step is to study in detail the differentiability properties of the control-to-state operator.
Due to these reasons, to prove first order necessary conditions, we are forced to work in the chemotaxis-free case, meaning that we put
\chi=0
and consider the system:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi-\Delta\mu=P(\varphi)(\sigma-\mu)-\mathbbm{h}(\varphi)u\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }Q_{T},

(1.7)

\displaystyle\mu=AF^{\prime}(\varphi)+Ba\varphi-BJ\ast\varphi\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }Q_{T},

(1.8)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma-\Delta\sigma=-P(\varphi)(\sigma-\mu)+v\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }Q_{T}.

(1.9)

\displaystyle\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}}\mu=\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}}\sigma=0\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma_{T},

(1.10)

\displaystyle\varphi(0)=\varphi_{0},\hskip 10.00002pt\sigma(0)=\sigma_{0}\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega.

(1.11)

Our aim is to study the following optimal distributed control problem:

(CP)
Minimise the cost functional

\begin{split}\mathcal{J}(\varphi,\sigma,u,v)&=\,\frac{\alpha_{\Omega}}{2}\int_{\Omega}|\varphi(T)-\varphi_{\Omega}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{\alpha_{Q}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\varphi-\varphi_{Q}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad+\frac{\beta_{\Omega}}{2}\int_{\Omega}|\sigma(T)-\sigma_{\Omega}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x+\frac{\beta_{Q}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\sigma-\sigma_{Q}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad+\frac{\alpha_{u}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|u|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\beta_{v}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|v|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t,\end{split}

(1.12)

subject to the control constraints

\begin{split}&u\in\mathcal{U}_{\text{ad}}:=\{u\in L^{\infty}(Q_{T})\mid u_{\text{min}}\leq u\leq u_{\text{max}}\text{ a.e.\penalty\ in }Q_{T}\},\\
&v\in\mathcal{V}_{\text{ad}}:=\{v\in L^{\infty}(Q_{T})\mid v_{\text{min}}\leq v\leq v_{\text{max}}\text{ a.e.\penalty\ in }Q_{T}\},\end{split}

(1.13)

and to the state system (
1.7
)-(
1.11
)
.

Here
\alpha_{\Omega},\alpha_{Q},\beta_{\Omega},\beta_{Q},\alpha_{u},\beta_{v}
are non-negative parameters that can be used to select which targets have to be privileged.
The function
\varphi_{\Omega}
is a final target for the tumour distribution, for instance one that could be suitable for surgery; whereas
\varphi_{Q}
is a possibly desired evolution.
In the same way,
\sigma_{\Omega}
and
\sigma_{Q}
are respectively a final target and a desired evolution for the nutrient.
Finally, the last two terms in the cost functional penalise large use of radiotherapy or chemotherapy, which could still harm the patient in the long run.
The aim of the optimal control problem is, then, to find the best therapies
u
and
v
, which can lead the evolution of the tumour to the desired targets.
Similar control problems for tumour growth models were studied in
[
12
,
28
,
13
,
11
,
9
]
, but we can still count few contributions in the non-local case (see
[
22
,
17
,
43
]
).
Regarding (CP), in Theorem
5.2
we show the existence of an optimal pair
(\overline{u},\overline{v})
.
Then, through careful analysis of the control-to-state operator and the introduction of the adjoint system, in Theorem
5.9
we prove the first-order necessary optimality conditions, which have to be satisfied by the optimal pair.
We stress that proving necessary optimality conditions is the first step needed in order to justify numerical treatment of the optimal control problem.
Indeed, the most commonly used algorithms are gradient descent schemes, where the descent direction is found through the optimality condition (see
[
45
, Section 3.7]
and references therein).

We finally mention that the present paper can be seen as a follow-up to
[
17
]
, where the same optimal control problem for a similar model was analysed.
The model in
[
17
]
is essentially the same as (
1.2
)–(
1.6
), up to an extra viscous regularisation term
\tau\partial_{t}\varphi
in (
1.3
) with
\tau>0
.
The viscous term made it easier to prove high regularity results for the relaxed system, even with singular potentials and chemotaxis, however this called for an additional constraint on the control
u
, namely it was necessary to assume that
u
was uniformly bounded in
H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
.
Such additional constraint is definitely not a desired one in practice, as it makes it harder to compute the
L^{2}
-orthogonal projection onto
\mathcal{U}_{\text{ad}}
, which is something that necessarily has to be done when solving numerically.
In the present case, not only are we able to solve the optimal control problem without the viscous relaxation, but we are also able to avoid asking additional regularity on
u
.
This is mainly due to our new strategy for proving high regularity for the solution of the tumour growth model, relying on maximal regularity theory.
We also mention that such theory was actually crucial in proving regularity results for our system in three dimensions.
Indeed, our initial attempts made use of more standard techniques, inspired by
[
19
]
, but they only got us a regular solution in two dimension without chemotaxis, while also assuming
u\in H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
.

The plan of the paper is the following.
In Section
2
, we state the notations and the main hypotheses on the parameters that will be used throughout the paper.
Then, we state a first result about existence of weak solutions for (
1.2
)–(
1.6
), which directly follows from the results proven in
[
21
]
.
In Section
3
, we start by introducing all the machinery of maximal regularity theory in weighted
L^{p}
spaces and apply it to prove the first main result of the paper: Theorem
3.10
about local existence and uniqueness of maximal solutions, even for
\chi>0
.
Moreover, we prove a technical result (Lemma
3.11
) about a time-regularisation effect due to the use of weighted spaces, which is the key ingredient allowing the extension to a global solution.
Section
4
is dedicated to proving strong well-posedness for the chemotaxis-free system (
1.7
)–(
1.11
).
First, starting from weak solutions, we prove global boundedness through an Alikakos-Moser iteration scheme (Proposition
4.1
) and a global Hölder regularity result (Proposition
4.5
).
Using these and the previous Lemma
3.11
, we hence prove the second main result of the paper about existence and uniqueness of global highly regular solutions (Theorems
4.7
and
4.8
).
Finally, in Theorem
4.11
we prove a strong continuous dependence result from data, which is the first key step in the analysis of the optimal control problem.
In Section
5
, we consequently study the optimal control problem (CP).
Indeed, we first prove existence of a solution in Theorem
5.2
.
Then, we consider the linearised system and prove the Fréchet-differentiability of the control-to-state operator in Theorem
5.5
.
In conclusion, we introduce the related adjoint system and deduce the first-order necessary optimality conditions in Theorem
5.9
, the third main result of the paper.

2
Preliminaries and existence of weak solutions

We now introduce some notation that will be used throughout the paper. We denote with
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
N=2,3
an open bounded domain with boundary
\partial\Omega
of class
\mathcal{C}^{2}
and exterior normal unit vector
\boldsymbol{n}
, whereas
T>0
is a fixed final time.
The
\mathcal{C}^{2}
requirement for
\partial\Omega
is needed for regularity estimates in Section
3
, while for weak solutions one can just assume that
\partial\Omega
is Lipschitz.
For convenience, we also denote
Q_{t}=\Omega\times(0,t)
and
\Sigma_{t}=\partial\Omega\times(0,t)
, for any
t\in(0,T]
.

Next, we recall the usual conventions regarding the Hilbertian triplet used in this context.
If we define

H=L^{2}(\Omega),\hskip 10.00002ptV=H^{1}(\Omega),\hskip 10.00002ptW=\{u\in H^{2}(\Omega)\mid\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}}u=0\text{ on }\partial\Omega\},

then we have the continuous and dense embeddings:

W\hookrightarrow V\hookrightarrow H\cong H^{*}\hookrightarrow V^{*}\hookrightarrow W^{*}.

We denote by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{V}
the duality pairing between
V^{*}
and
V
and by
(\cdot,\cdot)_{H}
the scalar product in
H
.
Regarding Lebesgue and Sobolev spaces, we will use the notation
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^{p}(\Omega)}
for the
L^{p}(\Omega)
-norm and
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{W^{k,p}(\Omega)}
for the
W^{k,p}(\Omega)
-norm, with
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq p\leq\infty
.
Moreover, we observe that, by elliptic regularity theory, an equivalent norm on
W
is

\lVert u\rVert^{2}_{W}=\lVert u\rVert^{2}_{H}+\lVert\Delta u\rVert^{2}_{H}.

Finally, we recall the Riesz isomorphism
\mathcal{N}:V\to V^{*}
:

\langle\mathcal{N}u,v\rangle_{V}:=\int_{\Omega}\left(\nabla u\cdot\nabla v+uv\right)\,\mathrm{d}x\hskip 10.00002pt\forall u,v\in V.

It is well-known that for
u\in W
we have
\mathcal{N}u=-\Delta u+u\in H
and that the restriction of
\mathcal{N}
to
W
is an isomorphism from
W
to
H
.
Additionally, by the spectral theorem, there exists a sequence of eigenvalues
0<\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\dots
, with
\lambda_{j}\to+\infty
, and a family of eigenfunctions
w_{j}\in W
such that
\mathcal{N}w_{j}=\lambda_{j}w_{j}
, which forms an orthonormal basis in
H
and an orthogonal basis in
V
.
In particular,
w_{1}
is constant.

Finally, we recall some useful inequalities that will be used throughout the paper:

•

Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality
. Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
bounded Lipschitz,
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
1\leq r,q\leq\infty
,
j\in\mathbb{N}
with
0\leq j\leq m
and
j/m\leq\alpha\leq 1
such that

\frac{1}{p}=\frac{j}{N}+\left(\frac{1}{r}-\frac{m}{N}\right)\alpha+\frac{1-\alpha}{q},

then

\lVert D^{j}f\rVert_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\,\lVert f\rVert^{\alpha}_{W^{m,r}(\Omega)}\lVert f\rVert^{1-\alpha}_{L^{q}(\Omega)}.

In particular, we recall the following versions with
N=2,3
:

\begin{split}&\lVert f\rVert_{L^{4}(\Omega)}\leq C\lVert f\rVert^{1/2}_{H^{1}(\Omega)}\lVert f\rVert^{1/2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\quad\text{ if }N=2,\\
&\lVert f\rVert_{L^{3}(\Omega)}\leq C\lVert f\rVert^{1/2}_{H^{1}(\Omega)}\lVert f\rVert^{1/2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\quad\text{ if }N=3.\end{split}

(2.1)

•

Agmon’s inequality
. Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
bounded Lipschitz,
0\leq k_{1}<N/2<k_{2}
and
0<\alpha<1
such that
N/2=\alpha k_{1}+(1-\alpha)k_{2}
, then

\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq C\,\lVert f\rVert^{\alpha}_{H^{k_{1}}(\Omega)}\lVert f\rVert^{1-\alpha}_{H^{k_{2}}(\Omega)}.

In particular, we recall the following versions with
N=2,3
:

\begin{split}&\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq C\lVert f\rVert^{1/2}_{H^{1}(\Omega)}\lVert f\rVert^{1/2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\quad\text{ if }N=2,\\
&\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq C\lVert f\rVert^{1/2}_{H^{2}(\Omega)}\lVert f\rVert^{1/2}_{H^{1}(\Omega)}\quad\text{ if }N=3.\end{split}

(2.2)

Note that all constants
C>0
mentioned above depend only on the measures of the sets and the parameters, not on the actual functions.

Now we introduce the structural assumptions on the parameters of our model (
1.2
)–(
1.6
), which we recall here for convenience:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi-\Delta\mu=P(\varphi)(\sigma+\chi(1-\varphi)-\mu)-\mathbbm{h}(\varphi)u\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }Q_{T},

\displaystyle\mu=AF^{\prime}(\varphi)+Ba\varphi-BJ\ast\varphi-\chi\sigma\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }Q_{T},

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\sigma-\Delta\sigma+\chi\Delta\varphi=-P(\varphi)(\sigma+\chi(1-\varphi)-\mu)+v\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }Q_{T},

\displaystyle\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}}\mu=\partial_{\boldsymbol{n}}(\sigma-\chi\varphi)=0\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma_{T},

\displaystyle\varphi(0)=\varphi_{0},\hskip 10.00002pt\sigma(0)=\sigma_{0}\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega.

We assume the following:

A1.

A,B>0
and
\chi\geq 0
.

A2.

J\in W^{1,1}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
is a symmetric convolution kernel, namely
J(z)=J(-z)
for any
z\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
. Moreover, we suppose that

a(x):=(J\ast 1)(x)=\int_{\Omega}J(x-y)\,dy\geq 0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{a.e. }x\in\Omega

and also that we have the bounds:

a^{\ast}:=\sup_{x\in\Omega}\,\int_{\Omega}\lvert J(x-y)\rvert\,dy<+\infty,\hskip 20.00003ptb^{\ast}:=\sup_{x\in\Omega}\,\int_{\Omega}\lvert\nabla J(x-y)\rvert\,dy<+\infty.

A3.

F\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and there exists
c_{0}>\chi^{2}\geq 0
such that

AF^{\prime\prime}(s)+Ba(x)\geq c_{0}\hskip 10.00002pt\forall s\in\mathbb{R}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{a.e. }x\in\Omega.

A4.

There exist
c_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
c_{2}>\frac{\chi^{2}}{A}
such that

F(s)\geq c_{2}\lvert s\rvert^{2}-c_{1}\hskip 10.00002pt\forall s\in\mathbb{R}.

A5.

Assume that there exist
z\in(1,2]
,
c_{3}>0
and
c_{4}\geq 0
such that

\lvert F^{\prime}(s)\rvert^{z}\leq c_{3}F(s)+c_{4}\hskip 10.00002pt\forall s\in\mathbb{R}.

A6.

P\in\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R})
and there exist
c_{5}>0
and
q\in[1,\frac{4}{3}]
such that

0\leq P(s)\leq c_{5}(1+\lvert s\rvert^{q})\hskip 10.00002pt\forall s\in\mathbb{R}.

A7.

\mathbbm{h}\in\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
.

A8.

u\in L^{\infty}(Q_{T})
and
v\in L^{2}(0,T;V^{*})
.

A9.

\varphi_{0}\in H
with
F(\varphi_{0})\in L^{1}(\Omega)
and
\sigma_{0}\in H
.

Finally, we would like to stress that, in the following, we will extensively use the symbol
C>0
to denote positive constants, which may change from line to line.
They will depend only on
\Omega
,
T
, the parameters and on the norms of the fixed functions introduced in hypotheses
A1
–
A9
and possible subsequent ones.
Sometimes, we will also add subscripts on
C
to highlight some particular dependences of these constants.

We now state a first result about existence of weak solutions to our system (
1.2
)–(
1.6
). We would like to point out that existence of weak solutions under assumptions
A1
–
A9
was already proved in
[
21
]
, when
u\equiv v\equiv 0
. In our case, the presence of
v
does not hinder the cited proof, since it can be easily treated. However, the term
-\mathbbm{h}(\varphi)u
requires some little changes in said proof, by following what was done in
[
17
, Remark 2.7]
. Below we give just a brief idea on how to modify the argument, by leaving most of the details to the interested reader.

Theorem 2.1
.

Under assumptions
A1
–
A9
, there exists a weak solution
(\varphi,\mu,\sigma)
to (
1.2
)–(
1.6
), such that

\displaystyle\varphi\in H^{1}(0,T;V^{*})\cap\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T],H)\cap L^{2}(0,T;V),

\displaystyle\mu\in L^{2}(0,T;V),

\displaystyle\sigma\in H^{1}(0,T;V^{*})\cap\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T],H)\cap L^{2}(0,T;V),

which satisfies

\varphi(0)=\varphi_{0}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\sigma(0)=\sigma_{0}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in }H

and the following variational formulation for a.e.
t\in(0,T)
and for any
\zeta\in V
:

\displaystyle\langle\varphi_{t},\zeta\rangle_{V}+(\nabla\mu,\nabla\zeta)_{H}=(P(\varphi)(\sigma+\chi(1-\varphi)-\mu),\zeta)_{H}-(\mathbbm{h}(\varphi)u,\zeta)_{H},

(2.3)

\displaystyle(\mu,\zeta)_{H}=(AF^{\prime}(\varphi)+Ba\varphi-BJ\ast\varphi-\chi\sigma,\zeta)_{H},

(2.4)

\displaystyle\langle\sigma_{t},\zeta\rangle_{V}+(\nabla\sigma-\chi\nabla\varphi,\nabla\zeta)_{H}=-(P(\varphi)(\sigma+\chi(1-\varphi)-\mu),\zeta)_{H}+\langle v,\zeta\rangle_{V}.

(2.5)

In particular, there exists a constant
C>0
, depending only on the parameters of the model and on the data
\varphi_{0}
,
\sigma_{0}
,
u
and
v
, such that:

\begin{split}&\lVert\varphi\rVert_{H^{1}(0,T;V^{*})\cap L^{\infty}(0,T,H)\cap L^{2}(0,T;V)}+\lVert\mu\rVert_{L^{2}(0,T;V)}\\
&\quad+\lVert\sigma\rVert_{H^{1}(0,T;V^{*})\cap L^{\infty}(0,T,H)\cap L^{2}(0,T;V)}\leq C.\end{split}

(2.6)

Proof.

The proof follows exactly the argument of
[
21
, Theorem 2.1]
, with one main difference due to the presence of the source terms. The main energy estimate is done by testing (
1.2
) by
\mu
, (
1.3
) by
-\varphi_{t}
and (
1.4
) by
\sigma+\chi(1-\varphi)
. Therefore, the two extra terms to treat are
-(\mathbbm{h}(\varphi)u,\mu)_{H}
and
\langle v,\sigma+\chi(1-\varphi)\rangle_{V}
. The second one can be easily treated by duality properties and Young’s inequality. Regarding the first one, instead, one can argue exactly as in the first part of
[
17
, Remark 2.7]
, by assuming
\tau=0
. Observe that, in this case, one also has to slightly modify the estimate from below of the energy
E(t)
as in
[
17
, Remark 2.7]
. In this way, the bound
F(\varphi)\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))
directly comes from the first energy estimate.

The rest of the procedure, also including the Galerkin approximation and the passage to the limit, is the same as
[
21
, Theorem 2.1]
, so we omit the details.
∎

3
Local Maximal
L^{p}_{\rho}
regularity

As already stated in the introduction, in order to prove further regularity for the solutions of our system (
1.2
)-(
1.6
), we use an approach based on maximal regularity theory in weighted
L^{p}
spaces. Before starting, we recall some notation and results introduced in the series of papers
[
37
,
38
,
39
]
. Then, we apply their results to prove local existence and uniqueness of maximal solutions. In our case, the difference from the previous works is that we are dealing with a system of equations containing also some space-dependent and time-dependent terms, as well as some external source terms. Therefore we have to make some adjustments, but the core of the argument is similar to the one of
[
23
]
.

3.1
Functional framework

We start by introducing the function spaces that we are going to use and by reformulating our problem (
1.2
)-(
1.6
) in a more suitable way.
Following
[
38
]
, for
p\in(1,+\infty)
,
\rho\in(1/p,1]
,
X
real Banach space and
T\in(0,+\infty)
, we introduce the time-weighted spaces:

\displaystyle L^{p}_{\rho}(0,T;X):=\Big\{f:(0,T)\to X\hbox{ strongly measurable such that }

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\lVert f\rVert^{p}_{L^{p}_{\rho}(0,T;X)}=\int_{0}^{T}t^{p(1-\rho)}\lVert f(t)\rVert_{X}^{p}\,\mathrm{d}t<+\infty\Big\},

\displaystyle W^{1,p}_{\rho}(0,T;X):=\left\{f\in L^{p}_{\rho}(0,T;X)\mid\partial_{t}f\in L^{p}_{\rho}(0,T;X)\right\}.

Observe that
\rho=1
yields the unweighted case, which means that
L^{p}_{1}=L^{p}
, moreover one can easily see that
L^{p}(0,T;X)\hookrightarrow L^{p}_{\rho}(0,T;X)
for any
\rho\in(1/p,1]
. Then, in a standard way, one can define the spaces
W^{k,p}_{\rho}(0,T;X)
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and, by real interpolation, also the fractional order spaces
W^{s,p}_{\rho}(0,T;X)
for any
s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, as done in
[
38
]
.

Remark 3.1
.

We observe that the temporal weight
t^{p(1-\rho)}
has a regularising effect only for
t=0
. Namely, if
f\in L^{p}_{\rho}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))
with
\rho\in(1/p,1]
, then
f\in L^{p}_{1}(s,T;L^{p}(\Omega))
for any
s>0
. Indeed:

+\infty>\int_{s}^{T}t^{p(1-\rho)}\lVert f\rVert^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\,\mathrm{d}t\geq\left(\min_{[s,T]}t^{p(1-\rho)}\right)\int_{s}^{T}\lVert f\rVert^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\,\mathrm{d}t\implies\int_{s}^{T}\lVert f\rVert^{p}_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\,\mathrm{d}t<+\infty.

However, this is enough to prove some useful time-regularisation estimates (see Lemma
3.11
).

From now on, we assume that the bounded domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
N=2,3
, has boundary of class
\mathcal{C}^{2}
. Next, we introduce the actual spaces we work with for our maximal regularity theory. Let
I=(0,T)
be a finite time-interval,
p\in(N+2,+\infty)
and
\rho\in(1/p,1]
. We define the maximal regularity class

E_{2,\rho}(I):=W^{1,p}_{\rho}(I;L^{p}(\Omega))\cap L^{p}_{\rho}(I;W^{2,p}(\Omega)),

the boundary class

F_{\rho}(I):=W^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2p},\,p}_{\rho}(I;L^{p}(\partial\Omega))\cap L^{p}_{\rho}(I;W^{1-\frac{1}{p},\,p}(\partial\Omega)),

as well as the starting space

E_{0,\rho}(I)=L^{p}_{\rho}(I;L^{p}(\Omega)).

Note that, for the boundary class, we are dealing with Neumann boundary conditions, therefore it is expected that the spaces have one degree less of regularity with respect to Dirichlet trace spaces.
We recall that, regarding these spaces, in
[
38
]
the following embedding results were proved:

E_{2,\rho}(I)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I},W^{2\left(\rho-\frac{1}{p}\right),\,p}(\Omega))\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I},\mathcal{C}^{1}(\overline{\Omega})),

(3.1)

where the last embedding holds if and only if
2\left(\rho-\frac{1}{p}\right)>1+\frac{N}{p}
. Moreover, if
2\left(\rho-\frac{1}{p}\right)>1+\frac{1}{p}
, we also have that

F_{\rho}(I)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I},W^{2\left(\rho-\frac{1}{p}\right)-1-\frac{1}{p},\,p}(\partial\Omega))\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I},\mathcal{C}^{0}(\partial\Omega)),

(3.2)

where again the last embedding holds if and only if
2\left(\rho-\frac{1}{p}\right)>1+\frac{N}{p}
.

Now, it is convenient to rewrite our system (
1.2
)-(
1.6
) in the following abstract form:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\uppsi}(x,t)+\mathcal{A}\left(x,t,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}(x,t)\right)=\boldsymbol{v}(x,t)\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }Q_{T},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}\left(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}(x,t)\right)=\boldsymbol{0}\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma_{T},

(3.3)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\uppsi}(x,0)=\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{0}(x)\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega,

where

\boldsymbol{\uppsi}(x,t)=\binom{\varphi(x,t)}{\sigma(x,t)},\hskip 10.00002pt\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{0}(x)=\binom{\varphi_{0}(x)}{\sigma_{0}(x)},\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{A}\left(x,t,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}\right)=\binom{\mathcal{A}_{1}(x,t,\varphi,\sigma)}{\mathcal{A}_{2}(x,\varphi,\sigma)},

with components given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}(x,t,\varphi,\sigma)=-\divergence((AF^{\prime\prime}(\varphi)+Ba(x))\nabla\varphi)+\chi\Delta\sigma-\divergence(B\nabla a(x)\,\varphi-B\nabla J\ast\varphi)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 10.00002pt-P(\varphi)(\sigma+\chi(1-\varphi)-AF^{\prime}(\varphi)-Ba(x)\,\varphi+BJ\ast\varphi+\chi\sigma)+\mathbbm{h}(\varphi)u(x,t),

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{2}(x,\varphi,\sigma)=-\Delta\sigma+\chi\Delta\varphi

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 10.00002pt+P(\varphi)(\sigma+\chi(1-\varphi)-AF^{\prime}(\varphi)-Ba(x)\,\varphi+BJ\ast\varphi+\chi\sigma)

and

\mathcal{B}\left(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}\right)=\binom{\nabla\varphi_{\mid\partial\Omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+l(x,\varphi_{\mid\partial\Omega})\left(B\varphi_{\mid\partial\Omega}\nabla a(x)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}-B(\nabla J\ast\varphi)_{\mid\partial\Omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}-\chi\nabla\sigma_{\mid\partial\Omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\right)}{\nabla\sigma_{\mid\partial\Omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}-\chi\nabla\varphi_{\mid\partial\Omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}},

with

0\leq l(x,s):=\frac{1}{AF^{\prime\prime}(s)+Ba(x)}\leq\frac{1}{c_{0}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for a.e. }x\in\Omega\text{ and any }s\in\mathbb{R},

and finally

\hskip 10.00002pt\boldsymbol{v}(x,t)=\binom{0}{v(x,t)}.

Remark 3.2
.

We want to stress that our operators
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
depend also on
x
and
t
, differently from what was done in
[
37
]
and
[
23
]
. However, the dependence on
x
is only through the function
a\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
(actually, we will have
a\in W^{2,q}(\Omega)
, for any
q>1
, with additional hypotheses on
J
; see Remark
3.7
in the next subsection). The dependence on
t
, instead, is only through the function
u(x,t)\in L^{\infty}(Q_{T})
, which appears only as a lower order term. Moreover, we also have a source term, which we will assume to be
\boldsymbol{v}\in L^{\infty}(Q_{T})^{2}
. Consequently, the high regularity of these terms allows us to proceed with similar arguments to the ones used in the cited papers, up to some adjustments.

Remark 3.3
.

Observe that, by hypothesis
A3
, the function
l(x,s)
is well-defined for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
and for a.e.
x\in\Omega
. Moreover, if
F\in\mathcal{C}^{4}(\mathbb{R})
, we also have that

l(x,\cdot)\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for a.e. }x\in\Omega.

We additionally remark that we chose to rewrite the first boundary condition in (
1.5
) by using the function
l(x,\varphi_{\mid\partial\Omega})
in order to highlight the structure of an inhomogeneous Robin boundary condition and to keep consistency with the notation used in
[
23
]
.

Finally, we introduce the sought regularity for the solutions of (
3.1
):

Definition 3.4
.

Assume that the initial data belong to the following space:

\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{0}\in M^{s,p}:=\{\boldsymbol{\uppsi}\in(W^{s,p}(\Omega))^{2}\mid\mathcal{B}(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi})=0\text{ a.e. on }\Omega\},

with

p\in(N+2,+\infty),\hskip 10.00002pt\rho\in\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{N+2}{2p},1\right],\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ and }s=2\left(\rho-\frac{1}{p}\right)>1+\frac{N}{p}.

We say that
\boldsymbol{\uppsi}
is a
maximal solution
to (
3.1
) on the interval
I=(0,T)
if it satisfies (
3.1
) almost everywhere in
Q_{T}
and

\boldsymbol{\uppsi}\in(E_{2,\rho}(I))^{2}\cap\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T);M^{s,p}).

Remark 3.5
.

One could also be interested in studying existence of maximal solutions for all times, i.e. for
T=+\infty
. Indeed, this can easily be included in our theory by considering weighted spaces with zero temporal trace in
t=0
. More details about this matter can be found in the already cited papers
[
37
,
39
,
23
]
. In particular, in
[
37
]
it was shown that, for this kind of zero temporal trace spaces, the constants of the embeddings
3.1
and
3.2
are independent of
T>0
. However, for simplicity we will stick with finite-time evolution.

3.2
Local-in-time existence

The aim of this subsection is to establish existence and uniqueness of maximal solutions to (
3.1
), in the sense of Definition
3.4
, at least locally in time, by adapting the results proved in
[
39
,
37
]
.
In order to prove this, we shall use maximal
L^{p}_{\rho}
-regularity results for the linearised problem associated to (
3.1
) and then apply the Banach contraction principle to get the same regularity also in the non-linear case. We observe that, as it is said in
[
37
, Remark 3.6]
, as long as the operators
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are of class
\mathcal{C}^{1}
and a version of the maximal
L^{p}_{\rho}
-regularity holds for the corresponding linearised problem, the proof of local existence and uniqueness is actually independent of the concrete form of the operators. Therefore, by looking at
[
39
, Theorem 2.1]
for maximal regularity for linear parabolic systems, we can include also our
(x,t)
-dependent operators in this setting.

We now need to assume stronger hypotheses on the parameters of our system (
1.2
)-(
1.6
), on top of the previous ones. In particular, we would need to assume that
J\in W^{2,1}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, but this hypothesis is incompatible with widely used convolution kernels, such as those of Newton or Bessel type.
However, following
[
5
, Definition 1]
, we can still introduce a suitable class of kernels, which includes the ones mentioned before and satisfies our needs.
Indeed, we recall the following definition:

Definition 3.6
.

A convolution kernel
J\in W^{1,1}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
is
admissible
if it satisfies the following conditions:

•

J\in\mathcal{C}^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{N}\setminus\{0\})
.

•

J
is radially symmetric and non-increasing, i.e.
J(\cdot)=\tilde{J}(\lvert\cdot\rvert)
for a non-increasing function
\tilde{J}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
.

•

There exists
R_{0}
such that
r\mapsto\tilde{J}^{\prime\prime}(r)
and
r\mapsto\tilde{J}^{\prime}(r)/r
are monotone on
(0,R_{0})
.

•

There exists
C_{N}>0
such that
\lvert D^{3}J(x)\rvert\leq C_{N}\lvert x\rvert^{-N-1}
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
.

Now, we can assume the following:

B1.

J\in W^{2,1}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
or
J
is
admissible
in the sense of Definition
3.6
.

B2.

F\in\mathcal{C}^{4}(\mathbb{R})
.

B3.

P\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
.

B4.

\mathbbm{h}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
.

B5.

u,v\in L^{\infty}(Q_{T})
.

B6.

\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{0}=\displaystyle\binom{\varphi_{0}}{\sigma_{0}}\in M^{s,p}
as in Definition
3.4
.

Remark 3.7
.

We recall that if
J
satisfies
B1
, then, by
[
5
, Lemma 2]
, for any
p\in(1,+\infty)
there exists a constant
b_{p}>0
such that:

\lVert\nabla(\nabla J\ast f)\rVert_{L^{p}(\Omega)^{3\times 3}}\leq b_{p}\lVert f\rVert_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\hskip 10.00002pt\forall f\in L^{p}(\Omega).

Moreover, by Young’s inequality for convolutions, this implies that
a\in W^{2,p}(\Omega)
for any
p\in(1,+\infty)
. In this way we can include typical choices for
J
, such as Newton or Bessel type potentials, which satisfy Definition
3.6
.

Before proving the main result, we state and prove two technical lemmas about the regularity of the operators
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
.

Lemma 3.8
.

Assume hypotheses
A1
-
A7
and
B1
-
B6
. Set
I=(0,T)
,
T>0
, and let
p\in(N+2,+\infty)
and
\rho\in\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{N+2}{2p},1\right]
.

Then for a.e.
(x,t)\in Q_{T}

\mathcal{A}(x,t,\cdot)\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2};E_{0,\rho}(I)^{2}),

and, for
\boldsymbol{\uppsi}\in E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}
, we have that for any
\boldsymbol{h}=(h,k)^{\top}\in E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}
and for a.e.
(x,t)\in Q_{T}

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(x,t,\boldsymbol{\uppsi})\boldsymbol{h}=\binom{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{1}(x,t,\varphi,\sigma)\boldsymbol{h}}{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{2}(x,\varphi,\sigma)\boldsymbol{h}},

where:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{1}(x,t,\varphi,\sigma)\boldsymbol{h}=-\divergence\left((AF^{\prime\prime}(\varphi)+Ba(x))\nabla h+AF^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi)\nabla\varphi\,h\right)+\chi\Delta k

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt-\divergence(B\nabla a(x)\,h-B\nabla J\ast h)-P^{\prime}(\varphi)h\sigma-P(\varphi)k-\chi(1-\varphi)P^{\prime}(\varphi)h

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\chi P(\varphi)h+AP^{\prime}(\varphi)F^{\prime}(\varphi)h+AP(\varphi)F^{\prime\prime}(\varphi)h+Ba(x)P(\varphi)h+Ba(x)\,P^{\prime}(\varphi)\varphi\,h

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt-BP(\varphi)(J\ast h)-BP^{\prime}(\varphi)(J\ast\varphi)h-\chi P^{\prime}(\varphi)h\sigma-\chi P(\varphi)k+\mathbbm{h}^{\prime}(\varphi)h\,u(x,t),

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\prime}_{2}(x,\varphi,\sigma)\boldsymbol{h}=-\Delta k+\chi\Delta h+P^{\prime}(\varphi)h\sigma+P(\varphi)k+\chi(1-\varphi)P^{\prime}(\varphi)h-\chi P(\varphi)h

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt-AP^{\prime}(\varphi)F^{\prime}(\varphi)h-AP(\varphi)F^{\prime\prime}(\varphi)h-Ba(x)P(\varphi)h-Ba(x)\,P^{\prime}(\varphi)\varphi\,h

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+BP(\varphi)(J\ast h)+BP^{\prime}(\varphi)(J\ast\varphi)h+\chi P^{\prime}(\varphi)h\sigma+\chi P(\varphi)k.

In particular, for
R>0
given, there exists a continuous function
\varepsilon:[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
,
\varepsilon(0)=0
, such that

\lVert\mathcal{A}(\cdot,\cdot,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}+\boldsymbol{h})-\mathcal{A}(\cdot,\cdot,\boldsymbol{\uppsi})-\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\cdot,\cdot,\boldsymbol{\uppsi})\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)^{2}}\leq\varepsilon(\lVert\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}})\lVert\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}},

(3.4)

for any
\boldsymbol{\uppsi},\boldsymbol{h}\in E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}
such that

\lVert\boldsymbol{\uppsi}\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I},\mathcal{C}^{1}(\overline{\Omega}))^{2}},\lVert\boldsymbol{\uppsi}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}},\lVert\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}}\leq R.

(3.5)

Proof.

We take inspiration from
[
23
, Lemma 3.2]
.
During the course of the proof, we will extensively use the following two facts:

•

W^{1,p}(\Omega)
is a
Banach algebra
if
p>N
; in particular the following property holds:

\lVert fg\rVert_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}\leq\lVert f\rVert_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}+\lVert f\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\lVert g\rVert_{W^{1,p}(\Omega)}\hskip 10.00002pt\forall f,g\in W^{1,p}(\Omega).

•

If
g:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
function, then
g:\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})^{m}\to\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})^{n}
,
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
as a Nemytskii operator. In particular, for any
R>0
there exists
\varepsilon:[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
non-decreasing, continuous and with
\varepsilon(0)=0
such that for any
\boldsymbol{\uppsi},\boldsymbol{h}\in\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})^{m}

\lVert g(\boldsymbol{\uppsi}+\boldsymbol{h})-g(\boldsymbol{\uppsi})-Dg(\boldsymbol{\uppsi})\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})^{n}}\leq\varepsilon(\lVert\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})^{m}})\lVert\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})^{m}}.

When applying this property, we always write
\varepsilon
, even if the actual function may change from line to line. In particular, we generally use this inequality in conjunction with the embedding
E_{2,\rho}(I)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})
, that is:

\lVert g(\boldsymbol{\uppsi}+\boldsymbol{h})-g(\boldsymbol{\uppsi})-Dg(\boldsymbol{\uppsi})\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})^{n}}\leq\varepsilon(\lVert\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)^{m}})\lVert\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)^{m}}.

(3.6)

Before starting, observe that, since
\varphi\in E_{2,\rho}(I)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})
and
F,P,\mathbbm{h}
are regular functions by
B2
,
B3
and
B4
, all the terms involving derivatives of
F
,
P
,
\mathbbm{h}
and their products, evaluated at
\varphi
, are bounded in
\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})
. Therefore, it is easy to see that
\mathcal{A}(x,t,\cdot):E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}\to E_{0,\rho}(I)^{2}
is well-defined, by also using hypothesis
B1
and Remark
3.7
. Moreover, it is clear that in order to show that
\mathcal{A}(x,t,\cdot)
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, we just need to show the differentiability estimate (
3.4
) and that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(x,t,\cdot)
is continuous.

So we start by proving (
3.4
). By noticing that for linear terms the following difference is identically equal to
0
, we are left to estimate:

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{A}(\cdot,\cdot,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}+\boldsymbol{h})-\mathcal{A}(\cdot,\cdot,\boldsymbol{\uppsi})-\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\cdot,\cdot,\boldsymbol{\uppsi})\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\divergence(AF^{\prime\prime}(\varphi+h)\nabla(\varphi+h))-\divergence(AF^{\prime\prime}(\varphi)\nabla\varphi)-\divergence(AF^{\prime\prime}(\varphi)\nabla h+AF^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi)\nabla\varphi\,h)\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+2\lVert(1+\chi)P(\varphi+h)(\sigma+k)-(1+\chi)P(\varphi)\sigma-(1+\chi)P^{\prime}(\varphi)\sigma\,h-(1+\chi)P(\varphi)k\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+2\lVert\chi P(\varphi+h)(1-\varphi-h)-\chi P(\varphi)(1-\varphi)-\chi P^{\prime}(\varphi)(1-\varphi)h-\chi P(\varphi)h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+2\lVert AP(\varphi+h)F^{\prime}(\varphi+h)-AP(\varphi)F^{\prime}(\varphi)-AP^{\prime}(\varphi)F^{\prime}(\varphi)h-AP(\varphi)F^{\prime\prime}(\varphi)h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+2\lVert BaP(\varphi+h)(\varphi+h)-BaP(\varphi)\varphi-BaP(\varphi)h-BaP^{\prime}(\varphi)\varphi h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+2\lVert BP(\varphi+h)(J\ast(\varphi+h))-BP(\varphi)(J\ast\varphi)-BP(\varphi)(J\ast h)-BP^{\prime}(\varphi)(J\ast\varphi)h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\lVert\mathbbm{h}(\varphi+h)u-\mathbbm{h}(\varphi)u-\mathbbm{h}^{\prime}(\varphi)h\,u\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle:=I_{1}+2I_{2}+2I_{3}+2I_{4}+2I_{5}+2I_{6}+I_{7}.

We now proceed term by term. For the first one, we explicitly compute the divergence operator and rearrange the terms, so that

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert AF^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi+h)\nabla(\varphi+h)\cdot\nabla(\varphi+h)-AF^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi)\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\varphi

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt-2AF^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi)\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla h-AF^{(4)}(\varphi)\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\varphi h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\lVert AF^{\prime\prime}(\varphi+h)\Delta(\varphi+h)-AF^{\prime\prime}(\varphi)\Delta\varphi-AF^{\prime\prime}(\varphi)\Delta h-AF^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi)\Delta\varphi h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert A(F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi+h)-F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi)-F^{(4)}(\varphi)h)\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\varphi\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\lVert 2A(F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi+h)-F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi))\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}+\lVert AF^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi+h)\nabla h\cdot\nabla h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\lVert A(F^{\prime\prime}(\varphi+h)-F^{\prime\prime}(\varphi)-F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi)h)\Delta\varphi\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}+\lVert A(F^{\prime\prime}(\varphi+h)-F^{\prime\prime}(\varphi))\Delta h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}.

At this point, we exploit estimate (
3.6
), hypothesis
B2
, which implies that
F^{\prime\prime}
and
F^{\prime\prime\prime}
are locally Lipschitz and the fact that
\varphi,h\in E_{2,\rho}(I)
, together with the embeddings (
3.1
), to infer that

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle\leq A\lVert F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi+h)-F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi)-F^{(4)}(\varphi)h\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I},\mathcal{C}^{1}(\overline{\Omega}))}\lVert\nabla\varphi\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+2A\lVert F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi+h)-F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi)\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I},\mathcal{C}^{1}(\overline{\Omega}))}\lVert\nabla h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+A\lVert F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi+h)\rVert_{\infty}\lVert h\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I},\mathcal{C}^{1}(\overline{\Omega}))}\lVert\nabla h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+A\lVert F^{\prime\prime}(\varphi+h)-F^{\prime\prime}(\varphi)-F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi)h\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\lVert\Delta\varphi\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+A\lVert F^{\prime\prime}(\varphi+h)-F^{\prime\prime}(\varphi)\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\lVert\Delta h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\leq(\lVert\varphi\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}+\lVert\varphi\rVert^{2}_{E_{2,\rho}(I)})\,\varepsilon(\lVert h\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)})\lVert h\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}+C(1+\lVert\varphi\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)})\lVert h\rVert^{2}_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}.

For the second term, since
P\in C^{1}
and therefore locally Lipschitz, we similarly deduce that

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\chi)\lVert P(\varphi+h)-P(\varphi)-P^{\prime}(\varphi)h\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\lVert\sigma\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+(1+\chi)\lVert P(\varphi+h)-P(\varphi)\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\lVert k\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\sigma\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}\,\varepsilon(\lVert h\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)})\lVert h\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}+C\lVert h\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}\lVert k\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}.

As one can readily see, under our hypotheses the terms
I_{3}
,
I_{4}
and
I_{5}
can be treated in the same way to get:

I_{3}+I_{4}+I_{5}\leq C(1+\lVert\varphi\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)})\,\varepsilon(\lVert h\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)})\lVert h\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}+C\lVert h\rVert^{2}_{E_{2,\rho}(I)},

where we used
A2
for the terms involving
a
.
Moreover, by using the local Lipschitz continuity of
P
, (
3.6
),
A2
and Young’s inequality for convolutions, we similarly deduce that:

\displaystyle I_{6}

\displaystyle\leq B\lVert P(\varphi+h)-P(\varphi)-P^{\prime}(\varphi)h\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\lVert J\ast\varphi\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+B\lVert P(\varphi+h)-P(\varphi)\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\lVert J\ast h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\leq C\lVert\varphi\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}\,\varepsilon(\lVert h\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)})\lVert h\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}+C\lVert h\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}\lVert h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}.

Finally, by using (
3.6
) and hypotheses
B4
and
B5
, we can also see that:

I_{7}\leq\lVert u\rVert_{L^{\infty}(Q_{T})}\lVert\mathbbm{h}(\varphi+h)-\mathbbm{h}(\varphi)-\mathbbm{h}^{\prime}(\varphi)h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}\leq C\varepsilon(\lVert h\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)})\lVert h\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}.

Then, by putting all together and using (
3.5
), as well as the trivial embedding
E_{2,\rho}(I)\hookrightarrow E_{0,\rho}(I)
, we obtain that for any
\boldsymbol{\uppsi},\boldsymbol{h}\in E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{A}(\cdot,\cdot,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}+\boldsymbol{h})-\mathcal{A}(\cdot,\cdot,\boldsymbol{\uppsi})-\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\cdot,\cdot,\boldsymbol{\uppsi})\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\leq(1+\lVert\boldsymbol{\uppsi}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}}+\lVert\boldsymbol{\uppsi}\rVert^{2}_{E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}})\,\varepsilon(\lVert\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}})\lVert\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}}+C(1+\lVert\boldsymbol{\uppsi}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}})\lVert\boldsymbol{h}\rVert^{2}_{E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\leq C_{R}\lVert\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}}\left(\varepsilon(\lVert\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}})+\lVert\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}}\right),

which implies the Fréchet-differentiability of
\mathcal{A}(x,t,\cdot):E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}\to E_{0,\rho}(I)^{2}
, as
\lVert\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}}\to 0
for a.e.
(x,t)\in Q_{T}
.

Now, to show that actually
\mathcal{A}(x,t,\cdot)\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2},E_{0,\rho}(I)^{2})
for a.e.
(x,t)\in Q_{T}
, it remains to prove that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(x,t,\cdot):E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}\to\mathcal{L}(E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2},E_{0,\rho}(I)^{2})
is continuous. Indeed, given
\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{1},\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{2}\in E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}
, for any
\boldsymbol{h}\in E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}
, we estimate:

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\cdot,\cdot,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{1})\boldsymbol{h}-\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\cdot,\cdot,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{2})\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\divergence(AF^{\prime\prime}(\varphi_{1})\nabla h-AF^{\prime\prime}(\varphi_{2})\nabla h)\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}+\lVert\divergence(AF^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi_{1})\nabla\varphi_{1}h-AF^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi_{2})\nabla\varphi_{2}h)\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+2\lVert(1+\chi)(P^{\prime}(\varphi_{1})\sigma_{1}h-P^{\prime}(\varphi_{2})\sigma_{2}h)\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}+2\lVert(1+\chi)(P(\varphi_{1})k-P(\varphi_{2})k)\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+2\lVert\chi(1-\varphi_{1})P^{\prime}(\varphi_{1})h-\chi(1-\varphi_{2})P^{\prime}(\varphi_{2})h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}+2\lVert\chi P(\varphi_{1})h-\chi P(\varphi_{2})h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+2\lVert A(PF^{\prime})^{\prime}(\varphi_{1})h-A(PF^{\prime})^{\prime}(\varphi_{2})h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}+2\lVert BaP(\varphi_{1})-BaP(\varphi_{2})\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+2\lVert BaP^{\prime}(\varphi_{1})\varphi_{1}h-BaP^{\prime}(\varphi_{2})\varphi_{2}h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}+2\lVert BP(\varphi_{1})(J\ast h)-BP(\varphi_{2})(J\ast h)\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+2\lVert BP^{\prime}(\varphi_{1})(J\ast\varphi_{1})h-BP^{\prime}(\varphi_{2})(J\ast\varphi_{2})h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}+\lVert u\rVert_{L^{\infty}(Q_{T})}\lVert\mathbbm{h}^{\prime}(\varphi_{1})h-\mathbbm{h}^{\prime}(\varphi_{2})h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle:=I_{1}+I_{2}+2I_{3}+2I_{4}+2I_{5}+2I_{6}+2I_{7}+2I_{8}+2I_{9}+2I_{10}+2I_{11}+I_{12},

where we note again that all the linear terms simplify when taking the difference. We argue term by term as before. Starting from the first one, by computing the divergence, adding and subtracting some terms and using
B2
and (
3.1
), we infer that

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle\leq A\lVert F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi_{1})(\nabla\varphi_{1}-\nabla\varphi_{2})\cdot\nabla h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}+A\lVert(F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi_{1})-F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi_{2}))\nabla\varphi_{2}\cdot\nabla h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+A\lVert(F^{\prime\prime}(\varphi_{1})-F^{\prime\prime}(\varphi_{2}))\Delta h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\leq A\lVert F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi_{1})\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\lVert\nabla\varphi_{1}-\nabla\varphi_{2}\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}\lVert h\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I},\mathcal{C}^{1}(\overline{\Omega}))}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+A\lVert F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi_{1})-F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\lVert\nabla\varphi_{2}\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}\lVert h\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I},\mathcal{C}^{1}(\overline{\Omega}))}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+A\lVert F^{\prime\prime}(\varphi_{1})-F^{\prime\prime}(\varphi_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\lVert\Delta h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\leq C(1+\lVert\varphi_{2}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)})\lVert h\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}\lVert\varphi_{1}-\varphi_{2}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}.

For
I_{2}
, we compute again the divergence and then, up to adding and subtracting some terms and using again
B2
and (
3.1
), we get:

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle\leq A\Big(\lVert F^{(4)}(\varphi_{1})\nabla\varphi_{1}\cdot(\nabla\varphi_{1}-\nabla\varphi_{2})h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}+\lVert F^{(4)}(\varphi_{2})\nabla\varphi_{2}\cdot(\nabla\varphi_{1}-\nabla\varphi_{2})h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\lVert(F^{(4)}(\varphi_{1})-F^{(4)}(\varphi_{2}))\nabla\varphi_{1}\cdot\nabla\varphi_{2}\,h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}+\lVert F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi_{1})(\Delta\varphi_{1}-\Delta\varphi_{2})h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\lVert(F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi_{1})-F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi_{2}))\Delta\varphi_{2}h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}+\lVert(F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi_{1})-F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi_{2}))\nabla\varphi_{1}\cdot\nabla h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\lVert F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi_{2})(\nabla\varphi_{1}-\nabla\varphi_{2})\cdot\nabla h\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq A\lVert h\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\Big(\lVert F^{(4)}(\varphi_{1})\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\lVert\varphi_{1}\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I},\mathcal{C}^{1}(\overline{\Omega}))}\lVert\nabla\varphi_{1}-\nabla\varphi_{2}\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\lVert F^{(4)}(\varphi_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\lVert\varphi_{2}\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I},\mathcal{C}^{1}(\overline{\Omega}))}\lVert\nabla\varphi_{1}-\nabla\varphi_{2}\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\lVert F^{(4)}(\varphi_{1})-F^{(4)}(\varphi_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\lVert\varphi_{1}\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I},\mathcal{C}^{1}(\overline{\Omega}))}\lVert\nabla\varphi_{2}\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\lVert F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi_{1})\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\lVert\Delta\varphi_{1}-\Delta\varphi_{2}\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}+\lVert F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi_{1})-F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\lVert\Delta\varphi_{2}\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\lVert h\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I},\mathcal{C}^{1}(\overline{\Omega}))}\lVert F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi_{1})-F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\varphi_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\lVert\nabla\varphi_{1}\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\lVert h\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I},\mathcal{C}^{1}(\overline{\Omega}))}\lVert F^{\prime\prime}(\varphi_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\lVert\nabla\varphi_{1}-\nabla\varphi_{2}\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\lVert\varphi_{1}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}+\lVert\varphi_{2}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}\right)\lVert h\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}\lVert\varphi_{1}-\varphi_{2}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+C\lVert\varphi_{1}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}\lVert\varphi_{2}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}\lVert F^{(4)}(\varphi_{1})-F^{(4)}(\varphi_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\lVert h\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}.

By similar strategies and by using
B3
, we can also deduce that

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\chi)\lVert h\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\left(\lVert P^{\prime}(\varphi_{1})\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\lVert\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2}\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}+\lVert P^{\prime}(\varphi_{1})-P^{\prime}(\varphi_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\lVert\sigma_{2}\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\lVert h\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}\lVert\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}+C\lVert\sigma_{2}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}\lVert h\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}\lVert P^{\prime}(\varphi_{1})-P^{\prime}(\varphi_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}.

In a similar way, under our hypotheses, we can also estimate all the other terms as

\displaystyle I_{4}+I_{5}+I_{6}+I_{7}+I_{8}+I_{9}+I_{10}+I_{11}+I_{12}\leq

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\lVert\varphi_{1}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}+\lVert\varphi_{2}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}\right)\lVert h\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}\lVert P^{\prime}(\varphi_{1})-P^{\prime}(\varphi_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+C\lVert h\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}\lVert(PF^{\prime})^{\prime}(\varphi_{1})-(PF^{\prime})^{\prime}(\varphi_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+C\lVert h\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}\lVert\mathbbm{h}^{\prime}(\varphi_{1})-\mathbbm{h}^{\prime}(\varphi_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}+C\lVert h\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)}\lVert\varphi_{1}-\varphi_{2}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)},

where, for
I_{10}
and
I_{11}
we also used Young’s inequality for convolutions.
Then, by putting all together and using (
3.5
), we see that

\displaystyle\lVert\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{1})-\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2},E_{0,\rho}(I)^{2})}=\sup_{\lVert\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}}=1}\lVert\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{1})\boldsymbol{h}-\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{2})\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{E_{0,\rho}(I)^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\leq C_{R}\Big(\lVert\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{2}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}}+\lVert F^{(4)}(\varphi_{1})-F^{(4)}(\varphi_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}+\lVert P^{\prime}(\varphi_{1})-P^{\prime}(\varphi_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+\lVert(PF^{\prime})^{\prime}(\varphi_{1})-(PF^{\prime})^{\prime}(\varphi_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}+\lVert\mathbbm{h}^{\prime}(\varphi_{1})-\mathbbm{h}^{\prime}(\varphi_{2})\rVert_{\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})}\Big)\to 0

as
\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{1}\to\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{2}
in
E_{2,\rho}(I)^{2}
(and therefore also in
\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{Q_{T}})^{2}
by (
3.1
)), since by
B2
,
B3
and
B4
we know that
F\in\mathcal{C}^{4}(\mathbb{R})
,
P\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathbbm{h}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
. Consequently, we have shown that
\mathcal{A}(x,t,\cdot):E_{2,\rho}(I)\to E_{0,\rho}(I)
is Fréchet-differentiable with continuity for a.e.
(x,t)\in Q_{T}
. This concludes the proof of Lemma
3.8
.
∎

Lemma 3.9
.

Assume hypotheses
A1
-
A7
and
B1
-
B6
. Set
I=(0,T)
,
T>0
, and let
p\in(N+2,+\infty)
and
\rho\in\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{N+2}{2p},1\right]
.

Then for a.e.
x\in\Omega

\mathcal{B}(x,\cdot)\in\mathcal{C}^{1}((E_{2,\rho}(I))^{2};(F_{\rho}(I))^{2}),

and, for
\boldsymbol{\uppsi}\in(E_{2,\rho}(I))^{2}
, we have that for any
\boldsymbol{h}=(h,k)^{\top}\in(E_{2,\rho}(I))^{2}

\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi})\boldsymbol{h}=\binom{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1}(x,\varphi,\sigma)\boldsymbol{h}}{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{2}(\varphi,\sigma)\boldsymbol{h}},

where:

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{1}(x,\varphi,\sigma)\boldsymbol{h}=\nabla{h}_{\mid\partial\Omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+l(x,\varphi_{\mid\partial\Omega})(B{h}_{\mid\partial\Omega}\nabla a(x)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}-B(\nabla J\ast{h})_{\mid\partial\Omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}-\chi\nabla{k}_{\mid\partial\Omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\hskip 20.00003pt\,\,\,+l^{\prime}(x,\varphi_{\mid\partial\Omega})(B\varphi_{\mid\partial\Omega}\nabla a(x)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}-B(\nabla J\ast\varphi)_{\mid\partial\Omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}-\chi\nabla\sigma_{\mid\partial\Omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}){h}_{\mid\partial\Omega},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{2}(\varphi,\sigma)\boldsymbol{h}=\nabla{k}_{\mid\partial\Omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}-\chi\nabla{h}_{\mid\partial\Omega}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}.

In particular, for
R>0
given, there exists a continuous function
\varepsilon:[0,+\infty)\to[0,+\infty)
,
\varepsilon(0)=0
, such that

\lVert\mathcal{B}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}+\boldsymbol{h})-\mathcal{B}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\uppsi})-\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{\uppsi})\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{(E_{0,\rho}(I))^{2}}\leq\varepsilon(\lVert\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{(E_{2,\rho}(I))^{2}})\lVert\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{(E_{2,\rho}(I))^{2}},

for any
\boldsymbol{\uppsi},\boldsymbol{h}\in(E_{2,\rho}(I))^{2}
such that

\lVert\boldsymbol{\uppsi}\rVert_{(\mathcal{C}(\overline{I},\mathcal{C}^{1}(\overline{\Omega})))^{2}},\lVert\boldsymbol{\uppsi}\rVert_{(E_{2,\rho}(I))^{2}},\lVert\boldsymbol{h}\rVert_{(E_{2,\rho}(I))^{2}}\leq R.

Proof.

We immediately observe that
\mathcal{B}_{2}
is linear, so there is nothing to prove regarding the second component. The only difficult part is the first component
\mathcal{B}_{1}
, but in this case the proof is essentially identical to the one of
[
23
, Lemma 3.3]
. Indeed, our function
l(x,\cdot)
, even if depending on
x
, has exactly the same properties as theirs, i.e. it is of class
\mathcal{C}^{2}
for a.e.
x\in\Omega
by Remark
3.3
. Moreover, the new terms depending on the presence of
a(x)
and
\chi
are essentially linear and can be treated in a very similar way to the convolution term, or actually in an easier way. For these reasons, we avoid going into the technical details here and we refer the interested reader to
[
23
, Lemma 3.3]
.
∎

At this point, we can state and prove our main result about existence and uniqueness of local maximal solutions.

Theorem 3.10
.

Let
s=2\left(\rho-\frac{1}{p}\right)>1+\frac{N}{p}
, with
\rho\in\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{N+2}{2p},1\right]
and
p\in(N+2,+\infty)
. Assume also hypotheses
A1
-
A7
and
B1
-
B6
.

Then, for any
\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{0}\in M^{s,p}
, there exists a time
t^{+}=t^{+}(\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{0},\boldsymbol{v})>0
such that (
3.1
) has a unique maximal solution in the sense of Definition
3.4
on
I=(0,t^{+})
. Moreover, the maximal time-interval of existence
I=[0,t^{+})
is such that either
t^{+}=T
or
t^{+}\in(0,T)
with
\lim_{t\to t^{+}}\lVert\boldsymbol{\uppsi}(t)\rVert_{(W^{s,p}(\Omega))^{2}}=+\infty
.

Proof.

We consider the linearised problem associated to (
3.1
) and first show that it enjoys maximal
L^{p}_{\rho}
-regularity for any
\boldsymbol{\uppsi}\in(E_{2,\rho}(I))^{2}
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\uppsi}\in(E_{2,\rho}(I))^{2}
be given and let
\boldsymbol{f}\in(E_{0,\rho}(I))^{2}
,
\boldsymbol{g}\in(F_{\rho}(I))^{2}
and
\boldsymbol{\upxi}_{0}\in M^{s,p}
, then consider the linearised problem:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\upxi}+\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\left(x,t,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}\right)\boldsymbol{\upxi}=\boldsymbol{f}\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }Q_{T},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\left(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}\right)\boldsymbol{\upxi}=\boldsymbol{g}\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma_{T},

(3.7)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\upxi}(x,0)=\boldsymbol{\upxi}_{0}(x)\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega.

We claim that there exists a unique maximal solution
\boldsymbol{\upxi}
to problem (
3.2
) such that

\lVert\boldsymbol{\upxi}\rVert_{(E_{2,\rho}(I))^{2}}\leq C\left(\lVert\boldsymbol{f}\rVert_{(E_{0,\rho}(I))^{2}}+\lVert\boldsymbol{g}\rVert_{(F_{\rho}(I))^{2}}+\lVert\boldsymbol{\upxi}_{0}\rVert_{(W^{s,p}(\Omega))^{2}}\right).

(3.8)

To show this, we want to prove that (
3.2
) enjoys maximal
L^{p}_{\rho}
-regularity, by applying
[
39
, Theorem 2.1]
, therefore we need to check its hypotheses. First, we observe that we can write:

(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}(x,t,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}),\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}))=(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}),\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}))+\text{ lower order terms, }

where the principal parts have the following form:

\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi})\boldsymbol{h}=-\divergence\left(\begin{pmatrix}AF^{\prime\prime}(\varphi)+Ba(x)&-\chi\\
-\chi&1\end{pmatrix}\binom{\nabla h}{\nabla k}\right),

\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi})\boldsymbol{h}=\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&-\chi\,l(x,\varphi_{\mid\partial\Omega})\\
-\chi&1\end{pmatrix}\binom{\nabla h\cdot\boldsymbol{n}}{\nabla k\cdot\boldsymbol{n}}\right).

Next, regarding the regularity of the top-order coefficients, i.e. those of the two
2\times 2
matrices above, we note that they all belong to
\mathcal{BUC}(\overline{I}\times\overline{\Omega};\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2})
, since
\varphi\in E_{2,\rho}(I)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I},\mathcal{C}^{1}(\overline{\Omega}))
is fixed and
a\in W^{2,p}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{1}(\overline{\Omega})
for
p>N+2
. Finally, the last conditions we need to check are normal ellipticity for
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}
and the Lopatinskii-Shapiro type condition for
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}},\tilde{\mathcal{B}})
. Both conditions follow easily, even in our
x
-dependent case, from
[
2
, Theorem 4.4 and Example 4.5]
, thanks to hypothesis
A3
. Observe that, to apply
[
2
, Theorem 4.4]
, it may be useful to rewrite
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
by multiplying the first row by
AF^{\prime\prime}(\varphi)+Ba(x)=l(x,\varphi)^{-1}
. Then, by
[
39
, Theorem 2.1]
, there exists a unique maximal solution
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in(E_{2,\rho}(I))^{2}
to (
3.2
) satisfying (
3.8
). Note that the time-dependent part of the operator
\mathcal{A}
is hidden in the lower order terms, therefore it does not hinder the ellipticity properties. Moreover, the statement of
[
39
, Theorem 2.1]
also allows time-dependent coefficients, as long as its hypotheses are satisfied.

At this point, since we have a unique maximal solution for the linearised system, we can employ the Banach contraction principle, exactly as in
[
37
, Lemma 3.2 and Lemma 3.3]
, to find the required maximal solution
\boldsymbol{\uppsi}
to (
3.1
). As already noted before, this procedure is independent of the concrete form of the operators, as long as they are
\mathcal{C}^{1}
and the maximal regularity holds for the linearised system. For the sake of brevity, we just give the main steps here, leaving most of the calculations to the detailed proof of
[
36
, Proposition 4.3.2]
. First, given
\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{0}\in M^{s,p}
, we can fix an extension
\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{*}\in E_{2,\rho}(I)
such that
\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{*}(\cdot,0)=\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{0}
, which exists by
[
38
, Lemma 4.3]
. Next, we consider the linear problem

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\upxi}+\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\left(x,t,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{*}\right)\boldsymbol{\upxi}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\left(x,t,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{*}\right)\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{*}-\mathcal{A}(x,t,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{*})+\boldsymbol{v}\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }Q_{T},

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\left(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{*}\right)\boldsymbol{\upxi}=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\left(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{*}\right)\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{*}-\mathcal{B}(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{*})\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{on }\Sigma_{T},

(3.9)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\upxi}(x,0)=\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{0}(x)\hskip 20.00003pt

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega,

and observe that both the right-hand sides
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\left(x,t,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{*}\right)\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{*}-\mathcal{A}(x,t,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{*})+\boldsymbol{v}\in E_{0,\rho}(I)
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\left(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{*}\right)\boldsymbol{\upxi}=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\left(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{*}\right)\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{*}-\mathcal{B}(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{*})\in F_{\rho}(I)
belong to the right spaces, due to Lemmas
3.8
and
3.9
, and also, since
\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{0})=0
, the compatibility condition
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{0})\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{0}=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{0})\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{0}-\mathcal{B}(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{0})
on
\partial\Omega
holds. Then maximal
L^{p}_{\rho}
regularity for (
3.2
), as above, yields a unique solution
\boldsymbol{\upxi}_{*}\in E_{2,\rho}(I)
of (
3.2
).
Now, using this
\boldsymbol{\upxi}_{*}
, we define for
\lambda,\tau\in(0,1]
the set

\Sigma(\lambda,\tau):=\left\{\boldsymbol{\uppsi}\in E_{2,\rho}(0,\tau)\mid\lVert\boldsymbol{\uppsi}-\boldsymbol{\upxi}_{*}\rVert_{E_{2,\rho}(0,\tau)}\leq\lambda,\,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}(\cdot,0)=\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{0}\right\},

which is closed in
E_{2,\rho}(0,\tau)
. At this point, for any
\boldsymbol{\uppsi}\in\Sigma(\lambda,\tau)
, we consider the linear system

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{\upxi}+\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\left(x,t,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{*}\right)\boldsymbol{\upxi}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\left(x,t,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{*}\right)\boldsymbol{\uppsi}-\mathcal{A}(x,t,\boldsymbol{\uppsi})+\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega\times(0,\tau),

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\left(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{*}\right)\boldsymbol{\upxi}=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\left(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{*}\right)\boldsymbol{\uppsi}-\mathcal{B}(x,\boldsymbol{\uppsi})

\displaystyle\text{on }\partial\Omega\times(0,\tau),

(3.10)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\upxi}(x,0)=\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega.

Exactly as above, also this linear system admits a unique solution
\boldsymbol{\upxi}=\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{\uppsi})\in E_{2,\rho}(0,\tau)
and this defines a map
\mathcal{S}:\Sigma(\lambda,\tau)\to E_{2,\rho}(0,\tau)
. Clearly,
\boldsymbol{\uppsi}\in\Sigma(\lambda,\tau)
solves our starting system (
3.1
) if and only if it is a fixed point of
\mathcal{S}
in
\Sigma(\lambda,\tau)
. Since, for given
\lambda
, each solution of (
3.1
) in
E_{2,\rho}(0,\tau)
belongs to
\Sigma(\lambda,\tau)
for sufficiently small
\tau
, our task is thus to show that the map
\mathcal{S}
has a unique fixed point in
\Sigma(\lambda,\tau)
, provided that
\lambda
and
\tau
are sufficiently small. To this end we use the Banach contraction principle.
Indeed, one has to show that
\mathcal{S}
maps
\Sigma(\lambda,\tau)
into itself for small
\lambda
and
\tau
, as well as that it is a contraction on
\Sigma(\lambda,\tau)
. These two properties can be verified exactly as in second and third steps of the proof of
[
36
, Proposition 4.3.2]
, by relying on the inequalities proven in Lemmas
3.8
and
3.9
and on properties of zero-temporal-trace spaces (see Remark
3.5
) to keep constants independent of
\tau
.
The existence of a maximal existence time
t^{+}=t^{+}(\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{0},\boldsymbol{v})
and a maximal solution in
\mathcal{C}([0;t^{+});W^{s,p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{N}))
then follows from standard arguments.
∎

We have shown that, under hypotheses
A1
-
A7
and
B1
–
B6
, our system (
1.2
)-(
1.6
) admits a unique maximal solution with regularity

\varphi,\sigma\in E_{2,\rho}(0,t^{+}):=W^{1,p}_{\rho}(0,t^{+};L^{p}(\Omega))\cap L^{p}_{\rho}(0,t^{+};W^{2,p}(\Omega)),

with
\rho\in\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{N+2}{2p},1\right]
and
p\in(N+2,+\infty)
, up to a certain maximal time
t^{+}=t^{+}(\varphi_{0},\sigma_{0})
. In the next section, we will extend this regularity to the whole interval
(0,T)
, however, in order to do this, it will be necessary to put
\chi=0
. Before going into the details, we need another technical lemma about a
\mathcal{C}^{\beta}-W^{2-2/p,p}
smoothing effect for the solutions to our problem, which will be useful in the next section. Note that this lemma can still be proven even if
\chi>0
.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
\rho=1
,
p\in(N+2,+\infty)
and
\boldsymbol{\uppsi}_{0}\in M^{2-2/p,p}
. Assume also hypotheses
A1
-
A7
and
B1
-
B5
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\uppsi}
be a maximal solution to (
3.1
) on
(0,t^{+})
in the sense of Definition
3.4
and let
t_{1},t_{2}\in(0,t^{+})
, with
t_{2}>t_{1}
and
\tau:=t_{2}-t_{1}
. Then, for any
\beta\in(0,1)
, there exists a constant
C>0
, depending only on the parameters of the system and
\tau
,
p
and
\delta_{\beta}=\lVert\boldsymbol{\uppsi}\rVert_{(\mathcal{C}([t_{1},t_{2}];\mathcal{C}^{\beta}(\Omega))^{2}}
, such that

\lVert\boldsymbol{\uppsi}(t_{2})\rVert_{(W^{2-2/p,p}(\Omega))^{2}}\leq C(1+\lVert\boldsymbol{\uppsi}(t_{1})\rVert_{(\mathcal{C}^{\beta}(\Omega))^{2}}).

Remark 3.12
.
